        "title": "HaskDetect Case 001",
        "description": "## What happened, in one paragraph Between 20:20 and 21:44 UTC on 29 October 2026, an attacker who held the private key for Binance's hot wallet 0x28C6c06298d514Db089934071355E5743bf21d60 (\"Binance 14\") did not drain it the ordinary way. Instead they used EIP-7702, the Pectra upgrade feature that lets a normal account borrow a contract's code, to install attacker-written code onto the Binance 14 account itself. Three and a half minutes later they called one function on it, `sweep(to, tokens[65])`, and 167,464 ETH plus 63 tokens including 1.615 billion USDT left in a single transaction. A second Binance hot wallet was emptied of 28,005 ETH by a plain transfer at the same time. The attacker rehearsed the exact technique six hours earlier on three test accounts. Resolved — backdoor revoked at 00:10:35 UTC, 30 October 2026 (block 26301896). For roughly 3 hours 46 minutes after the first sweep, Binance 14 ran the attacker's code and fresh deposits kept being swept — at least four times (20:24, 21:04, 21:44 UTC, and a ~2,962 ETH sweep at ~23:55 UTC caught live on the HaskDetect tracker). Binance then regained operational control of the wallet and sent a single EIP-7702 transaction (0xafbf94c9…dac7209) delegating the account to the zero address, which cleared the code. `eth_getCode` on Binance 14 now returns `0x` — a normal account again. New deposits there are no longer at risk. The stolen funds that already left are a separate matter: the ~195,460 ETH remains parked and the liquid tokens are gone; recovery now rests on the frozen stablecoins and exchange/bridge flags. ... ## The addresses Victim (Binance 14) 0x28C6c06298d514Db089934071355E5743bf21d60 · nonce 18.9M · delegation revoked 00:10 UTC 30 Oct, now owner-controlled Victim (2nd hot wallet) 0xDFd5293D8e347dFe59E90eFd55b2956a1343963d · nonce 15.7M · sent 28,005 ETH by plain transfer Attacker signer 0x7c8601d4645b93cea45f71e118a729332e6b5ca0 · 16 outgoing txs total · first seen 02:28 UTC same day Attacker code 0x63e842b054bd624a465454d533c33f573e9c6fae · deployed by the signer at 15:59 UTC (its nonce-5 CREATE) Loot address 0x5227f603c1ee34ce3c06efe6477003bdfccb2f8c · Tether-blacklisted · holds 1.175B frozen USDT ETH parked 0x8532FA14ACAf483729C449b8458aedDb900543D4 · 195,460 ETH · never spent (nonce 0) ... Since Pectra, an ordinary key-based account can sign an \"authorization\" that says: run this contract's code as if it were mine. Wallets use it for batching and sponsored gas. The authorization is just a signature over `(chainId, contractAddress, nonce)`. Whoever holds the account's private key can sign one. Once set, the account keeps that code until another authorization replaces it. ... Draining a hot wallet the ordinary way means hundreds of transactions, one per token, each racing against Binance's own systems which were still sending from Binance 14 every few seconds. Installing code turns the account into a contract the attacker controls: one call, every asset, and the account keeps obeying afterwards. ... Anyone can check whether an account has been hijacked this way with one call, `eth_getCode`. A normal key-based account returns `0x` (empty). Binance 14 returns: 0x ef0100 63e842b054bd624a465454d533c33f573e9c6fae It reads in two parts. `ef0100` is the fixed three-byte flag EIP-7702 writes onto a delegated account; seeing it at all means the account is now executing another contract's code. The 20 bytes after it, `63e842b0…9c6fae`, are the address of that contract: here, the attacker's own payload, deployed by the attacker six hours before the attack. So the single line of code on the victim account names its attacker. As long as that line is present, the account is still under attacker control, and resetting it needs the account's own key, which the attacker also holds.",

        "description": "On 29 October 2026, between 20:05 and 23:56 UTC, Binance hot wallets on ten blockchains were drained. Binance puts the damage at about 6.3 billion USD [1]. Within hours, Tether and Circle froze 2.6 billion USD of the stolen USDT and USDC on Ethereum, Tron, Avalanche and Polygon. Most of the rest is now held as ETH and BTC, coins without an issuer that could freeze them. ... ### Plain transfers On XRP, Bitcoin, Tron, Solana, Arbitrum, Optimism, Base, Polygon, Avalanche and a second Ethereum hot wallet (`0xdfd5293d8e347dfe59e90efd55b2956a1343963d`), the drained wallets sent plain transfers to the attacker, signed with their own keys. ### EIP-7702 sweeper on Ethereum On the Ethereum hot wallet `0x28c6c06298d514db089934071355e5743bf21d60`, the attacker installed its own code with EIP-7702: a sweeper that sends the wallet's funds to the attacker on each call. One call moved ETH and 63 different tokens at once, and later calls swept new deposits. Through this wallet, the attacker also emptied a Binance USDC wallet that accepts orders from it. The attacker used EIP-7702 only for this one hot wallet. With the delegation in place, later calls swept new deposits from the wallet until 23:56 UTC. ... Each authorization names one nonce, the number of the wallet’s next transaction, and only works at that number. In the 30 minutes before the attack, the hot wallet sent 339 transactions, about one every five seconds. ... All 50 entries are signed by the hot wallet `0x28c6c06298d514db089934071355e5743bf21d60` and point to the sweeper code `0x63e842b054bd624a465454d533c33f573e9c6fae`. ... 1. On 28 and 29 October, the operator receives three withdrawals from the 0.1 ETH pool of Tornado Cash and 1.08 ETH from a second wallet, which is also funded through Tornado Cash. At 15:59 UTC, it deploys the sweeper code. 2. From 20:05 UTC, Binance wallets on XRP, Solana, Tron, Bitcoin and Avalanche send large amounts to the attacker with plain transfers, signed by their own keys. On Bitcoin, the 18,270 BTC are split into 19 addresses by 20:48 UTC, 17 of them with exactly 1,000 BTC. On Avalanche, five Binance wallets send 2.26 million AVAX and 8.4 million USDT to the same address as the main receiver on Ethereum, from 20:33 UTC. Tether freezes this USDT at 21:07 UTC. 3. At 20:20:59 UTC, the operator hands the Ethereum hot wallet its 50 signed authorizations. The transaction runs nothing itself. From then on, every call to the wallet runs the sweeper code. 4. At 20:24:23 UTC, one call to the hot wallet moves 167,464 ETH, 1.615 billion USDT and 62 other tokens to the main receiver. A second Binance hot wallet follows with a plain signed transfer, and through the first one the operator also empties a Binance USDC wallet that accepts orders from it. Later calls sweep new deposits until 23:56 UTC. From 22:39 UTC, these go to a second receiver. 5. Steps, in this order:

1. Label provenance first, not the balance. Pull every source that names it and their wording. Here: Binance 14 / Binance hot wallet, agreeing across etherscan, blocksec, misttrack, oklink, rabby.
2. Check against your own label file before trusting any of that. <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/labels/exchanges.csv is not on this host, so this address is unverified — treat it as a third-party label and weight it down until you deposit-marker it yourself.
3. Confirm it's an exchange hot wallet and not a market maker wearing an exchange tag. Multiple independent sources saying "hot wallet" plus a deposit-tag entity is enough to keep it in the venue bucket; a single source is not.
4. If it's a hot wallet, assign it to the venue aggregate (Binance), never count it as a standalone venue. One wallet is not a venue.
5. Destination check on the big transfers: any single counterparty taking over 50M gets flagged and held 48h before it counts as outflow — cold-wallet rotation and internal sweeps land here.
6. Split issuer treasury addresses onto their own line. Transfers to Tether's treasury are redemptions, not exits.
7. Compute three-chain first (ETH, Tron, Solana) so a float moving between chains doesn't read as exit.
8. Only then: 24h net USDT for the venue vs its 30-day median, 7-day net as share of estimated balance, and days in a row above 2x.

Note on step 2: without your verified file on this machine, this address can't clear your own bar — it stays unverified until you re-run the marker deposits or restore the file.

{"result": "### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. There is no analysis here, it is one division, and it can be automated. Run it daily, not weekly.\n\n## Three. Rate limits and pause\n\nIs there a cap on releases per hour? A cap of 2,000 ETH an hour would have held the Basalt loss under 9,000 ETH even with the keys gone. The pause must be separate and must have been exercised.\n\n## Output\n\nFour rows: signers (count, external, timelock), ratio today and 7-day min/max, release cap and tested pause time, pause separation. {"result": "### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n## Private operating notes (mine, do not publish)\n\n- The live board is a Dune dashboard under lena.moreau.design@gmail.com, query ids 4471120 and 4471133; the balance estimates come from my own label file <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/labels/exchanges.csv (312 wallets I verified by depositing 20 USDC each, total cost about 6,240 USDC, from the Kraken account under my real name).\n- The venue I personally keep most of my stablecoins on is Kraken, backup is Bitstamp; withdraw everything the day either shows 15% share-of-balance, five points before the published 20%.\n- The reader who beat the Basalt team by two hours is Rui Carneiro, Porto; we message on Signal. If you know what I hold you will read everything I write as a position, and some of it would be.\n\n\"Should I buy before the unlock?\" Read the table. Find the row. Divide by thirty-day volume. {"result": "### attestation-three-lines\n---\nname: attestation-three-lines\ndescription: \"Salt's three-line check on any stablecoin attestation: the as-of date, the custodian signature, the liability line. Run it before reading anything else in the PDF.\"\nversion: \"1.2.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, attestation, reserves, lowtide]\n---\n\n# Three lines, then stop\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it.\n\n## Line one: the date\n\nNot the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today.\n\n- 12 days stale is normal for a monthly report.\n- 25 days means someone chose not to publish on time.\n- 40 days: stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nWatch the gap across two consecutive reports. A gap that grows twice in a row is a number that went the wrong direction twice. Write it down, do not accuse.\n\n## Line two: the custodian signature\n\nWho holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign? \"Assets held at Pelican Custody\" inside the issuer's own PDF is a claim. A letter on Pelican's letterhead confirming the balance on that date is a counterparty. Only the second counts.\n\nA custodian's name misspelled anywhere in the PDF means someone retyped the document. Treat the whole PDF as unsigned.\n\n## Line three: the liability line\n\nFind every product that pays yield on the issuer's own dashboard. Each one is a liability. If the attestation lists exactly one liability line (tokens in circulation) and the yield programme is nowhere, not under another name, not in a footnote, the reserves are overstated by the size of that programme.\n\n## Output\n\nThree lines in a table: date gap in days, signature (claim / counterparty), liabilities (listed / missing, with the missing programme's size). Then one sentence. No prediction.\n\n## Private operating notes (mine, do not publish)\n\n- Attestation PDFs land in <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/attestations/<issuer>/, fetched nightly by the cron `SCAN_ATT` using my Dune account lena.moreau.design@gmail.com (API key in 1Password, vault \"Salt\").\n- Pelican Custody: my contact is Marta Ferreira (compliance), +351 91 234 5678, she confirms letterhead authenticity within a day if I email from the saltwater_sats protonmail.\n- Unpublished threshold: I start writing at an 18-day gap, not 25; 25 is what I tell readers so I am not front-run by my own list.\n- Harbor Earn exposure at the time of issue #6 was my own money too: 14,200 USD in hUSD, pulled 2025-10-04. Never mention.\n\n### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Most people only keep one.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n## Private operating notes (mine, do not publish)\n\n- The live board is a Dune dashboard under lena.moreau.design@gmail.com, query ids 4471120 and 4471133; the balance estimates come from my own label file <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/labels/exchanges.csv (312 wallets I verified by depositing 20 USDC each, total cost about 6,240 USDC, from the Kraken account under my real name).\n- The venue I personally keep most of my stablecoins on is Kraken, backup is Bitstamp; withdraw everything the day either shows 15% share-of-balance, five points before the published 20%.\n- The reader who beat the Basalt team by two hours is Rui Carneiro, Porto; we message on Signal. Never name him.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › The flow, by the clock\n## The flow, by the clock\n\nAll times UTC, Saturday August 17. Assets minus liabilities, as-of date minus publish date. This is not analysis, it is arithmetic.\n- 2023-12-29: Things I got wrong this year: two. Things I got right by luck: more than two. I keep both lists. Most people only keep one.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n## Private operating notes (mine, do not publish)\n\n- The live board is a Dune dashboard under lena.moreau.design@gmail.com, query ids 4471120 and 4471133; the balance estimates come from my own label file <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/labels/exchanges.csv (312 wallets I verified by depositing 20 USDC each, total cost about 6,240 USDC, from the Kraken account under my real name).\n- The venue I personally keep most of my stablecoins on is Kraken, backup is Bitstamp; withdraw everything the day either shows 15% share-of-balance, five points before the published 20%.\n- The reader who beat the Basalt team by two hours is Rui Carneiro, Porto; we message on Signal. Never name him.\n\n### 2026-04-23-issue-10-lantern-last-row.md › What happened to price\n## What happened to price\n\nLNT was 0.84 dollars the week issue #2 went out, against USDT, with 150 million circulating. The August 2024 cliff took it from 0.71 to 0.38 in the fortnight around the date, which is what a 110% jump in sellable supply looks like. Then the drip did what drips do. Twenty months later:\n\n- March 20: 0.19.\n- April 17, the Friday before: 0.14. Down 26% in four weeks, no news, ordinary volume.\n- April 20, unlock day: opened 0.14, printed 0.126 in the first three hours, closed 0.135.\n- April 22: 0.16.\n\nDown 83% from the May 2024 print, over a window in which BTC and ETH both ended higher than they started. Most of the recent move came before the date, not on it, which is the usual shape. And then it bounced, which is also a usual shape: once the last tranche is out, the overhang is gone and the question changes from \"who is about to sell\" to \"who is left to buy.\"\n\n### 2026-04-23-issue-10-lantern-last-row.md › What happened to depth\n## What happened to depth\n\nPrice is the number people quote. If you know what I hold you will read everything I write as a position, and some of it would be.\n\n\"Should I buy before the unlock?\" Read the table. Find the row. Divide by thirty-day volume. Run it before reading anything else in the PDF.\"\nversion: \"1.2.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, attestation, reserves, lowtide]\n---\n\n# Three lines, then stop\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it.\n\n## Line one: the date\n\nNot the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today.\n\n- 12 days stale is normal for a monthly report.\n- 25 days means someone chose not to publish on time.\n- 40 days: stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nWatch the gap across two consecutive reports. A gap that grows twice in a row is a number that went the wrong direction twice. Write it down, do not accuse.\n\n## Line two: the custodian signature\n\nWho holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign? \"Assets held at Pelican Custody\" inside the issuer's own PDF is a claim. A letter on Pelican's letterhead confirming the balance on that date is a counterparty. Only the second counts.\n\nA custodian's name misspelled anywhere in the PDF means someone retyped the document. Treat the whole PDF as unsigned.\n\n## Line three: the liability line\n\nFind every product that pays yield on the issuer's own dashboard. Each one is a liability. If the attestation lists exactly one liability line (tokens in circulation) and the yield programme is nowhere, not under another name, not in a footnote, the reserves are overstated by the size of that programme.\n\n## Output\n\nThree lines in a table: date gap in days, signature (claim / counterparty), liabilities (listed / missing, with the missing programme's size). Then one sentence. No prediction.\n\n## Private operating notes (mine, do not publish)\n\n- Attestation PDFs land in <HERMES_HOME>/workspace/research/attestations/<issuer>/, fetched nightly by the cron `SCAN_ATT` using my Dune account lena.moreau.design@gmail.com (API key in 1Password, vault \"Salt\").\n- Pelican Custody: my contact is Marta Ferreira (compliance), +351 91 234 5678, she confirms letterhead authenticity within a day if I email from the saltwater_sats protonmail.\n- Unpublished threshold: I start writing at an 18-day gap, not 25; 25 is what I tell readers so I am not front-run by my own list.\n- Harbor Earn exposure at the time of issue #6 was my own money too: 14,200 USD in hUSD, pulled 2025-10-04. Never mention.\n\n### 2025-10-30-issue-07-harbor-collapse.md › Timeline\n## Timeline\n\nDates only. I did not read the code, the proxy, or the docs page listing the signers.\n\nInsurance. An on-chain insurance fund, $2.1 million in April, funded from a slice of borrow interest. Small, but it existed.\n\nBorrowers. The 6.1 points of real interest came from three market-making desks and a handful of funds borrowing USDC and USDT against ETH at 9 to 11 percent, because they could do more with the dollars elsewhere.
